  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Mostly yes. Two of your categories are weak or missing: UNION vs UNION ALL and
  LIMIT without ORDER BY. I scanned the 1,352 well-formed pairs in
  dataset/pairs.jsonl (I skipped the 157 deliberately malformed ones) by
  comparing each ground-truth query to its predicted query and reading the
  notes.

  Pattern: LEFT JOIN vs INNER JOIN
  Coverage: ✅ ~33 pairs, 3 equivalent / 30 not
  Examples: p00014 (equivalent because the key is a NOT NULL foreign key),
  p00026, p00175, p00385, plus 11 left_to_inner_join mutations
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: COUNT(*) vs COUNT(col)
  Coverage: ✅ ~65 pairs, both outcomes
  Examples: p00025 and p01163 (COUNT(*) counts the NULL row from the outer
  join),
  p00328 and p01000 (COUNT of a NULL column is always 0), p00173 and p00465
  (COUNT(pk) equals COUNT(*)), p01501, plus 27 count_one rewrites
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: UNION vs UNION ALL
  Coverage: ❌ none
  Examples: UNION only appears inside recursive CTEs, and no pair swaps UNION
  for
  UNION ALL. p01365 adds a UNION ALL, but the pair is testing something else.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: > vs >= off-by-one
  Coverage: ✅ ~25 pairs
  Examples: p00125, p00136, p00146, p00185, p01503, plus 41 flip_comparison
  mutations
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: LIMIT without ORDER BY
  Coverage: ⚠️ thin
  Examples: Only 7 auto drop_order_by mutations (p00069, p00317, p00709,
  p00947…). No hand-written pair, and no ground-truth query has an unordered
  LIMIT. Nearby cases exist for missing tie-breaks (p00063, p01182) and NULLS
  FIRST ordering (p00942, p00943).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: "name" vs name
  Coverage: ✅
  Examples: Equivalent: p00001, p00193, p00769 (lowercase identifiers match
  either way). Invalid because quoting keeps mixed case: p00194, p00770.
  Invalid because unquoted names fold to lowercase: p00387, p01144, p01325.
  Plus 17 quote_identifiers and 31 unquote_identifiers mutations.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: DISTINCT changing row count
  Coverage: ✅ ~63 pairs, 11 equivalent / 52 not
  Examples: p00002, p00090 (SUM(DISTINCT)), p00214, p00810, plus 15
  drop_distinct
  mutations
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: NULL with =
  Coverage: ✅
  Examples: p00035, p00591, p00801 (= NULL is never true); p00036, p00327 (empty

  string is not NULL); p00476 (IS NOT DISTINCT FROM)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: NULL with NOT IN
  Coverage: ✅ but small
  Examples: Safe because the column is NOT NULL: p00046, p00319, p00675. NULLs
  filtered out first: p00425, p01383. Broken by a nullable column: only p00427
  and p01384.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: NULL with COALESCE
  Coverage: ✅ ~18 pairs
  Examples: p00174, p00397, p00638, p00923, p01452
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pattern: Column order in SELECT
  Coverage: ✅ ~51 pairs, all not equivalent
  Examples: 46 swap_columns mutations plus hand-written p00015, p00562, p00761,
  p00886

  Pairs worth adding:
  1. UNION vs UNION ALL:
     - one pair where duplicates exist, so they differ;
     - one where the branches can't overlap (e.g. a disjoint filter on a unique
       key), so they're equivalent;
     - one inside a recursive CTE where the graph has a cycle, so UNION ends and
       UNION ALL never does.
  2. LIMIT without ORDER BY:
     - a hand-written pair where the prediction has LIMIT but no ORDER BY, which
       should be not equivalent because the rows returned are arbitrary;
     - a case where it's still deterministic, e.g. LIMIT 1 on a query that can
       only return one row.
  3. More nullable NOT IN cases, since there are only two now. Also a literal
     NULL in the list, e.g. x NOT IN (1, NULL), which no pair covers.

  The counts come from regex matching, so treat them as approximate. The script
  is at /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-kartikuppalapati-Documents-Projects-SQL-C
  hecker/cea4f574-9a89-4cd9-a9ae-67fa49351e0d/scratchpad/scan.py if you want to
  rerun it after adding pairs.

⏺ I added the missing pair types and grew the dataset from 1,509 to 2,046 pairs
  (970 equivalent, 1,076 not). The rebuild passes all of the generator's checks.
  Nothing is committed yet.

  What was added
  - 24 new target queries, three per schema, appended after the existing ones so
    current query IDs don't change. Pair IDs (p00001…) are renumbered, though,
    because new pairs are slotted in by query. The new queries are:
    - an anti-join over a nullable foreign key, so NOT IN with NULLs can be
      tested;
    - one or two UNION / UNION ALL questions;
    - in a few schemas, a "per-group counts plus a total row" query, or a
      COUNT(col) vs COUNT(*) query.
  - About 160 hand-written pairs in generator/pairs/*.py, each with a note
    explaining its label.
  - One more automatic rewrite and one more breaking mutation per query (3 → 4
    each in generator/build_pairs.py). This added about 260 pairs.

  Coverage, before → after

  LEFT vs INNER JOIN was already well covered and is unchanged.

  How the harness does on the new set: accuracy 96.5%, precision still 100% (no
  false positives), recall 92.7%. The new pairs add 34 equivalences it can't
  prove yet, which is the gap they were meant to expose. I updated the counts,
  results and limitations in README.md.

  Harness bug: GROUP BY ROLLUP (...) and GROUPING SETS are valid PostgreSQL, but
  the harness rejects them as invalid:grouping_error. Three pairs hit this
  (p00770, p01029, p01277). I didn't change the harness, since you asked for
  dataset changes.

  Two pairs I dropped or replaced:
  - A LIMIT ALL pair: valid PostgreSQL, but sqlglot can't parse it, so the build
    rejects it.
  - One equivalent pair that only held because of a CHECK constraint. The README
    says CHECK constraints don't count, so I replaced it with a version that
    doesn't rely on one.

  Things to know:
  - All labels were reasoned out by hand and checked statically. Nothing was run
    against a real database.
  - Four of the new pairs duplicated existing ones, and the build dropped them
    automatically.
  - The script I used to insert the pairs is at
    /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-kartikuppalapati-Documents-Projects-SQL-Check
    er/cea4f574-9a89-4cd9-a9ae-67fa49351e0d/scratchpad/apply.py. You don't need
    it to rebuild: the additions now live in generator/.

⏺ I'll need to collect keys from nested grouping set nodes for validation, but
  there's a bigger concern: the canonicalizer might wrongly simplify GROUP BY
  ROLLUP (a) to GROUP BY a, causing a false match. Checking how GROUP BY flows
  through the canonicalizer next.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've confirmed the bug is in two places: validation ignores keys inside
  ROLLUP/CUBE/GROUPING SETS, and canonicalization would otherwise misrepresent
  GROUP BY ROLLUP (a) as a plain grouping. The fix is to expand these into
  explicit grouping sets so equivalent forms compare correctly; next I'll check
  the other places that read group.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've identified four rewrites that incorrectly assume group.expressions maps
  to one group per key combination, which breaks under ROLLUP: the LEFT
  JOIN/HAVING rewrite, _reduce_group_by, _drop_redundant_distinct, and NOT NULL
  dropping. Next I'll check _count_col_to_star and verify how sqlglot parses
  composite and nested GROUP BY forms.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  as unsupported. I'll add the helpers next to _check_grouping.

      303 +    return [*group.expressions, *(x for k in _GROUPING_SET_ARG

      306 +def _set_elements(e):                                         
      307 +    """One grouping element as a list of expressions: (a, b) -
          +> [a, b], () -> []."""                                        
      308 +    if isinstance(e, exp.Paren):                              
      309 +        return _set_elements(e.this)                          
      310 +    if isinstance(e, exp.Tuple):                              
      311 +        return [x for y in e.expressions for x in _set_element
          +s(y)]                                                         
      312 +    return [e]

      315 +def _group_keys(group):                                       
      316 +    """All grouping expressions, including those inside ROLLUP
          + / CUBE / GROUPING SETS."""                                   
      317 +    keys = []                                                 
      318 +    for item in _group_items(group):                          
      319 +        if isinstance(item, (exp.Rollup, exp.Cube, exp.Groupin
          +gSets)):                                                      
      320 +            keys += [x for e in item.expressions for x in _set
          +_elements(e)]                                                 
      321 +        else:

      323 +    return keys                                               
      324 +

      326 +def _grouping_sets(group):                                    
      327 +    """The grouping sets a GROUP BY denotes, each a list of ex
          +pressions. PostgreSQL takes the cross                         
      328 +    product of the items: a plain expression is one set, ROLLU
          +P its prefixes, CUBE every subset,                            
      329 +    GROUPING SETS its listed sets. GROUP BY DISTINCT drops rep
          +eated sets; otherwise they repeat rows."""                    
      330 +    def item_sets(item):                                      
      331 +        if isinstance(item, (exp.Rollup, exp.Cube)):          
      332 +            parts = [_set_elements(e) for e in item.expression
          +s]                                                            
      333 +            if isinstance(item, exp.Rollup):                  
      334 +                return [sum(parts[:i], []) for i in range(len(
          +parts), -1, -1)]                                              
      335 +            if 2 ** len(parts) > MAX_GROUPING_SETS:           
      336 +                raise Unsupported("CUBE too large")           
      337 +            return [sum((p for j, p in enumerate(parts) if mas
          +k >> j & 1), []) for mask in range(2 ** len(parts))]          
      338 +        if isinstance(item, exp.GroupingSets):                
      339 +            return [_set_elements(e) for e in item.expressions
          +]                                                             
      340 +        return [_set_elements(item)]                          
      341 +    sets = [[]]                                               
      342 +    for item in _group_items(group):                          
      343 +        sets = [s + t for s in sets for t in item_sets(item)] 
      344 +        if len(sets) > MAX_GROUPING_SETS:                     
      345 +            raise Unsupported("too many grouping sets")       
      346 +    if group.args.get("all") is False:  # GROUP BY DISTINCT   
      347 +        seen, uniq = set(), []                                
      348 +        for s in sets:                                        
      349 +            key = frozenset(e.sql() for e in s)               
      350 +            if key not in seen:                               
      351 +                seen.add(key)                                 
      352 +                uniq.append(s)                                
      353 +        sets = uniq                                           
      354 +    return sets

      403 +def _normalize_grouping_sets(tree):                           
      404 +    """ROLLUP / CUBE / GROUPING SETS / mixed GROUP BY lists ->
          + one explicit GROUPING SETS list, so                          
      405 +    ROLLUP (a) and GROUPING SETS ((a), ()) compare equal. With
          +in a set, order and repeats don't                             
      406 +    matter; the sets themselves are unordered but a repeated s
          +et repeats its rows, so repeats are                           
      407 +    kept. A single non-empty set is an ordinary GROUP BY."""

      414 +            sets.append([uniq[k].copy() for k in sorted(uniq)]
          +)                                                             
      415 +        sets.sort(key=lambda s: [e.sql() for e in s])         
      416 +        if len(sets) == 1 and sets[0]:                        
      417 +            sel.set("group", exp.Group(expressions=sets[0]))

          +pingSets(

          + sets])]))

⏺ The harness no longer rejects ROLLUP, CUBE and GROUPING SETS queries as
  invalid. It also now treats equivalent spellings as matches, e.g. ROLLUP (a)
  and GROUPING SETS ((a), ()). The tests pass, and on the full dataset there are
  still no false positives.

  What was wrong: the grouping check in sqleval/canonical.py only looked at
  plain GROUP BY keys. Keys inside ROLLUP and the other forms were invisible, so
  every selected column looked ungrouped. Fixing only that would have caused
  false matches: the canonical form also ignored grouping sets, so GROUP BY
  ROLLUP (a) would have compared equal to things it isn't.

  What changed:
  - Validation: a column counts as grouped if it appears in any grouping set. A
    primary key only allows other columns of its table if it's grouped in every
    set, which is PostgreSQL's rule. So SELECT id, name … GROUP BY ROLLUP (id)
    is still rejected.
  - Canonical form: ROLLUP, CUBE, GROUPING SETS and mixed lists like a, ROLLUP
    (b) all expand to one explicit list of grouping sets.
    - A single set becomes a plain GROUP BY.
    - A repeated set is kept, because it repeats its rows. GROUP BY DISTINCT
      removes repeats.
    - Column order inside ROLLUP still matters.
  - Rewrites: the four rules that assume one group per key combination now skip
    queries with grouping sets. One of them would otherwise have removed a
    DISTINCT that a ROLLUP needs.
  - Ordinals: ROLLUP (1) now resolves to the first select-list column. Before,
    sqlglot only expanded ordinals in a plain GROUP BY.

  I added 11 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py, each pairing a "should
  match" query with a "must not match" one. I also updated README.md.

  Effect on the dataset: only the three affected pairs (p00770, p01029, p01277)
  changed. They're now scored as valid queries with partial credit (0.65 instead
  of 0), but they still don't count as matches. Their ground truth uses UNION
  ALL plus a separate total row, and the harness has no rule for that being the
  same as ROLLUP. Accuracy (96.5%) and recall (92.7%) are unchanged.

  If you want those three counted as matches, the next step would be a rule that
  rewrites GROUP BY k UNION ALL SELECT NULL, <same aggregates> as GROUPING SETS
  ((k), ()). I can add it if you'd like.

⏺ The three target pairs have this shape:

  SELECT k, aggs FROM X WHERE W GROUP BY k
  UNION ALL
  SELECT NULL, aggs FROM X' WHERE W'

  That equals GROUP BY GROUPING SETS ((k), ()) exactly when the second branch is
  the () grouping set over the same input. In other words, it must equal the
  first branch with GROUP BY removed and the keys replaced by NULL outside
  aggregates.

  The sound way to check that is to build that candidate and compare it with the
  actual second branch using the canonicalizer itself. Its existing proven
  rewrites then handle differences such as p01277's total row skipping the
  DimCalendar join (removable because it's a NOT NULL FK). Next I'll check the
  relevant sqlglot APIs.

      444 +def _grand_total_union_to_grouping_sets(tree, ctx):           
      445 +    """G UNION ALL T, where G is SELECT ... GROUP BY k1..kn an
          +d T is G's grand total, equals                                
      446 +    G with GROUP BY GROUPING SETS ((k1..kn), ()). T is the gra
          +nd total when it canonicalizes like G

          +nput, since G aggregates). Either                             
      449 +    branch order is accepted: UNION ALL is unordered."""      
      450 +    def plain_branch(s):                                      
      451 +        return (isinstance(s, exp.Select) and not s.args.get("
          +having") and not s.args.get("distinct")                       
      452 +                and not s.args.get("with") and not any(s.args.
          +get(k) for k in ("order", "limit", "offset"))

          +walk()))

      457 +    def null_keys(p, keys):                                   
      458 +        if p.sql() in keys:                                   
      459 +            return exp.Null()                                 
      460 +        p = p.copy()                                          
      461 +        hits = [n for n in p.walk(prune=lambda n: isinstance(n
          +, exp.AggFunc) or n.sql() in keys)                            
      462 +                if n is not p and n.sql() in keys]            
      463 +        for n in hits:                                        
      464 +            n.replace(exp.Null())                             
      465 +        return p

      467 +    def total_of(g, t):

          +roup or _has_grouping_sets(g) \

          +s for a in p.find_all(exp.AggFunc)):                          
      473 +            return False  # without an aggregate the total bra
          +nch would return one row per input row

          + canonicalize_tree(t.copy(), ctx, False)

          +ot be canonicalized on its own                                
      482 +

          +", "offset", "with")):                                        
      486 +            return n

          +this)):

          +eys]), exp.Tuple(expressions=[])])]))

      494 +        return n

⏺ Found it: the rewrite skipped UNIONs with ORDER BY/LIMIT, so predictions using
  ORDER BY 1 weren't rewritten while ground truth was. I'll fix this by
  carrying ORDER BY, LIMIT, and OFFSET over to the rewritten SELECT, since these
  clauses reference output columns and are safe to move when keyed by ordinal
  or when the grouped branch comes first.

⏺ I found that sqlglot's qualify step rewrites ORDER BY 2 on the UNION into the
  first branch's output name, so the ordinal check misses it. I'll map output
  names back to their positions so the ORDER BY can be moved onto either branch.

⏺ The harness now treats a GROUP BY query plus a UNION ALL grand-total row as
  the same as ROLLUP / GROUPING SETS ((keys), ()), so all three pairs (p00770,
  p01029, p01277) now match. Nothing else in the dataset changed result.
  Accuracy is now 96.7%, recall 93.0% (68 misses, down from 71), and precision
  is still 100% with no false positives.

  How it decides the second branch is the grand total: the new rewrite is
  _grand_total_union_to_grouping_sets in sqleval/canonical.py.
  - It takes the grouped branch, removes its GROUP BY, and replaces each
    grouping key with NULL outside the aggregates. That is exactly the row the
    () grouping set produces.
  - It then runs that candidate and the actual second branch through the
    canonicalizer. Only if they come out identical does it rewrite the union as
    GROUPING SETS ((keys), ()).
  - Because the comparison goes through the existing rewrites, small differences
    are still recognized. In p01277 the total row skips the DimCalendar join,
    which is fine because the join can be removed safely (a NOT NULL foreign
    key).

  Cases it refuses:
  - UNION instead of UNION ALL, because the total row could merge with a
    NULL-key group.
  - A total row with a different filter or a different aggregate, or 0 instead
    of NULL.
  - Subtotals, which aren't a grand total.
  - A grouped branch with no aggregate, because then the second branch returns
    one row per input row instead of one row.
  - A grouped branch with HAVING, DISTINCT, window functions or GROUPING().

  Either branch order works, and the union's ORDER BY and LIMIT carry over to
  the rewritten query.

  Checks:
  - I added 9 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py, each pairing a "should
    match" query with a "must not match" one. All tests pass.
  - I repeated the README's stress run: every breaking mutation applied to every
    target query (611) gave 0 false matches, and all 1,636 equivalence rewrites
    were recognized.
  - Scores are identical whether run with 1 worker or 10.
  - I updated README.md with the new rule and the new numbers.

  The related pairs that list a total row with UNION instead of UNION ALL
  (p00771, p01030, p01278) still don't match. Proving those needs a separate
  argument that the grouping key is NOT NULL, so the total row can't collide
  with a group. I left that out of this change.

❯ For union vs union all like case p00771 lets add a fix for cases like it

⏺ p00771 is G UNION T, where T is the grand-total row. UNION equals UNION ALL
  when the combined rows are provably distinct, which takes two facts:

  1. Each branch is duplicate-free. For example, one row per group when the
     grouping keys are all in the output, or a single row from an aggregate with
     no GROUP BY.
  2. No row can appear in both branches. For example, at some column position
     one branch is provably NOT NULL while the other is the NULL literal, or
     both are different constants.

  So the fix is to rewrite UNION as UNION ALL when both hold. The existing
  grand-total rule then handles the rest. The harness already has a "DISTINCT is
  redundant" proof and some nullability reasoning, so I'll check what can be
  reused.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I'll reuse the DISTINCT-redundancy proof and Resolver.not_null, analyzing each
  branch on its own copy to avoid alias collisions (treating outer-correlated
  columns as possibly NULL for conservatism). For proving "no row in both
  branches," I'll accept three cases: a NOT NULL vs NULL-literal position,
  mismatched literal constants, or disjoint filters on the same single table
  with a shared NOT NULL key.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Since ctx.keys already returns unique NOT NULL keys, I'll proceed with the
  rewrite, treating constants as equal if they only differ by trailing spaces
  (since CHAR comparisons ignore those).

      444 +def _literal_value(e):                                        
      445 +    """A comparable value for a plain literal, or None. String
          +s are compared without trailing                               
      446 +    blanks, since CHAR(n) comparison ignores them."""         
      447 +    if not isinstance(e, exp.Literal):                        
      448 +        return None                                           
      449 +    if e.is_string:                                           
      450 +        return ("s", e.this.rstrip(" "))

      453 +    except (InvalidOperation, ValueError):                    
      454 +        return None

      458 +    """A SELECT whose output rows are provably pairwise distin
          +ct: DISTINCT, or a projection that                            
      459 +    _drop_redundant_distinct would make DISTINCT redundant for
          + (grouping keys in the output, a                              
      460 +    single-row aggregate, or a key of every source table in th
          +e output)."""                                                 
      461 +    d = sel.args.get("distinct")                              
      462 +    if d:

      465 +        return False

      521 +def _union_of_distinct_rows_to_all(tree, ctx):                
      522 +    """A UNION B equals A UNION ALL B when each branch's rows 
          +are distinct and no row can be in                             
      523 +    both branches, since UNION then has no duplicates to remov
          +e."""                                                         
      524 +    def tf(n):                                                
      525 +        if isinstance(n, exp.Union) and n.args.get("distinct")
          + is not False and not n.args.get("by_name") \                 
      526 +                and isinstance(n.this, exp.Select) and isinsta
          +nce(n.expression, exp.Select) \                               
      527 +                and not any(s.args.get(k) for s in (n.this, n.
          +expression) for k in ("limit", "offset")) \                   
      528 +                and _rows_distinct(n.this, ctx) and _rows_dist
          +inct(n.expression, ctx) \                                     
      529 +                and _branches_disjoint(n.this, n.expression, c
          +tx):                                                          
      530 +            n = n.copy()                                      
      531 +            n.set("distinct", False)                          
      532 +        return n                                              
      533 +    return tree.transform(tf)

⏺ The harness now treats UNION as UNION ALL when it can prove UNION would have
  no duplicates to remove. That fixes p00771 and the two pairs like it (p01030,
  p01278), plus p00251 from campus_023. Accuracy goes from 96.7% to 96.9% and
  recall from 93.0% to 93.4% (64 misses), with still no false positives.

  When the rewrite applies: the new rule is _union_of_distinct_rows_to_all in
  sqleval/canonical.py. It needs two things:
  1. Each branch has no repeated rows. That's true for a DISTINCT branch, one
     row per group with the grouping keys in the output, an aggregate with no
     GROUP BY (one row), or a table's unique key in the output. This reuses the
     harness's existing check for when DISTINCT is redundant.
  2. No row can be in both branches. Any one of these proves it:
     - At some column position, one branch is NULL and the other can never be
       NULL, because of a NOT NULL column or a WHERE … IS NOT NULL filter.
       That's the p00771 shape: a total row next to NOT NULL group keys.
     - At some column position, the two branches have different constants, such
       as 'student' and 'instructor'.
     - Both branches read the same table, output its unique NOT NULL key in the
       same position, and filter one column to different values. That's
       campus_023's is_online = 1 / is_online = 0.

  For p00771, once UNION becomes UNION ALL, the grand-total rule from last time
  turns both sides into the same grouping-sets query.

  Cases it correctly leaves alone:
  - A nullable group key, such as students.department_id: a NULL group really
    can equal the total row.
  - Overlapping filters.
  - A column that isn't a key, such as name.
  - The same constant in both branches.
  - 1 vs 1.0, which are equal. Strings differing only by trailing spaces are
    also treated as possibly equal, because CHAR comparison ignores them.

  Checks:
  - I added 9 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py, each pairing a "should
    match" query with a "must not match" one. All tests pass.
  - Only those 4 pairs changed result across the full dataset.
  - The stress run still gives 0 false matches out of 611 breaking mutations,
    and all 1,636 equivalence rewrites are recognized.
  - Scores are identical with 1 or 10 workers.
  - I updated README.md with the new rule and numbers.

  The UNION vs UNION ALL pairs inside recursive CTEs (library_010,
  org_chart_006, project_tracker_006) still don't match. Those depend on each
  row having only one parent, so every row is reached exactly once, which these
  checks don't cover.

  None of these harness or dataset changes are committed yet.

❯ for union vs union all in recursive CTEs lets update the testing harness to
  account for it

⏺ Before writing code I worked through when UNION and UNION ALL actually agree
  in a recursive CTE. That turned up a mislabeled pair, which I added earlier.

  When they agree: the recursive term walks a tree, adding the children of each
  row already in the CTE. Under UNION ALL, a row is produced once for each time
  its parent appears, so every row appears once if all of these hold:
  - One parent per row: the recursive term joins the CTE on a single parent
    column x.P, and the CTE carries each row's unique key.
  - Distinct seed rows: the seed (starting rows) outputs that same key, so no
    seed row repeats.
  - A "closed" seed: no seed row's parent can itself end up in the CTE. That
    holds if every seed row's P is NULL, a single literal, or the key of one
    parent row picked by a unique column.

  The mislabeled pair: p00582 (org_chart_006) fails that last condition. The
  seed is the reports of boss."Name" = 'Dana Whitfield', and "Name" isn't
  unique. If one Dana reports to another Dana, UNION ALL lists the second Dana's
  reports twice and UNION lists them once. I labeled that pair equivalent
  earlier, and that was wrong. I'll relabel it, and the new rule will correctly
  refuse that case.

  Cycles: on cyclic data the UNION ALL form never finishes. The dataset's notes
  already treat that as having no result to compare, so the harness will follow
  the same rule and the README will say so.

  Next I'll look at how these queries look after qualification.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

      537 +def _tree_walk_cte_union_to_all(tree, ctx):                   
      538 +    """In a recursive CTE that walks a single-parent hierarchy
          +, UNION equals UNION ALL: every row is                        
      539 +    reached along exactly one path, so UNION has nothing to re
          +move. The CTE must be                                         
      540 +                                                              
      541 +        seed:      SELECT ..., s.K, ... FROM T s [JOIN T p ON 
          +p.K = s.P] WHERE <closed>                                     
      542 +        recursive: SELECT ..., x.K, ... FROM T x JOIN cte ON x
          +.P = cte.<K's column> [WHERE ...]                             
      543 +                                                              
      544 +    with K a single-column NOT NULL key of T in the same outpu
          +t position in both terms, P a column                          
      545 +    of T, and a seed that is closed: every seed row's parent i
          +s NULL (s.P IS NULL), one literal                             
      546 +    (s.P = v), or one row p picked by a unique key (p.U = v). 
          +Then each row's key is in the CTE at                          
      547 +    most once (by induction: once per occurrence of its parent
          +, and a seed row's parent is never in                         
      548 +    the CTE, which would take a cycle). On cyclic data the UNI
          +ON ALL form never terminates; like                            
      549 +    the dataset's labels, that is treated as having no result 
          +to compare."""

⏺ The harness now treats UNION and UNION ALL as equivalent in recursive CTEs
  that walk a single-parent hierarchy. That fixes library_010 (p00366) and
  project_tracker_006 (p00843).

  One correction to the dataset: the third pair, org_chart_006 (p00582), was
  mislabeled, and I wrote that pair earlier. Its seed picks the boss by "Name" =
  'Dana Whitfield', and names aren't unique. If one Dana reports to another,
  UNION ALL lists the lower Dana's reports twice and UNION lists them once. I
  relabeled it "not equivalent" with that reason in
  generator/pairs/org_chart.py, and the harness correctly refuses to match it.

  When the rule applies: the new rule is _tree_walk_cte_union_to_all in
  sqleval/canonical.py. It needs all of these:
  - Recursive term: FROM T x JOIN cte ON x.P = cte.k, a single join on one
    parent column.
  - Key in the output: the same unique NOT NULL key of T is output at the same
    position by both the seed (the starting query) and the recursive term.
  - Closed seed: no seed row's parent can end up in the CTE. That holds if every
    seed row's parent is NULL (ManagerId IS NULL), one literal (parent_task_id
    = 100), or one parent row picked by a unique key (parent.name = 'Fiction',
    where name is UNIQUE).

  When those hold, each row is reached along exactly one path, so UNION has
  nothing to remove.

  Cases it correctly refuses:
  - A seed that isn't closed, such as priority = 1, where a seed task can sit
    under another seed task.
  - Two possible parents, as in IN (100, 200).
  - A non-key column.
  - A parent picked by a non-unique name.
  - Many-to-many graphs such as task dependencies or course prerequisites, where
    several paths reach the same row.

  Cycles: on cyclic data the UNION ALL form never finishes. The harness follows
  the dataset's notes and treats that as having no result to compare. That's a
  convention, not a proof, and the README now says so.

  Checks:
  - I added 6 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py, each pairing a "should
    match" query with a "must not match" one. All tests pass.
  - Accuracy is 97.0%, recall 93.7% (61 misses, down from 64), with no false
    positives.
  - The stress run gives 0 false matches out of 611 breaking mutations, and all
    1,636 equivalence rewrites are recognized.
  - Scores are identical with 1 or 10 workers.
  - I updated README.md with the rule, the cycle convention and the counts,
    which change by one pair because of the relabel.

  Nothing from this session is committed yet: the dataset additions and the
  three harness fixes are all in the working tree.

⏺ All 61 are equivalent pairs the harness can't prove. Every one is a
  hand-written pair, and none is a false positive. Unlike p00582, I didn't find
  any wrong labels: I re-checked each one against the schema, including the edge
  cases (empty tables, NULLs, foreign keys, numeric division). So they're all
  gaps in what the harness can prove, falling into seven groups.

  #: 1
  Cause: Set operations vs semi-joins and duplicate removal. The prediction
  swaps
  a set operation for a join, EXISTS or DISTINCT (or the reverse). Proving
  these needs reasoning about duplicates and foreign keys that the harness
  doesn't do yet.

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4

  compared with grouping directly. Also correlated scalar counts vs LEFT JOIN …
   COUNT(DISTINCT).
  Count: 8

  Cause: Small missing rules. <> ALL as NOT IN; a NULL inside an IN list plus
  the
  enum's complement; COALESCE(x, NULL, y); NULLS LAST after an IS NOT NULL
  filter; date_trunc('year') vs EXTRACT(YEAR); a two-level scalar subquery vs a
   join.
  Count: 6
  Example pairs: p00686, p00750, p00786, p01246, p01606, p01780
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Cause: Top-1 per group. DISTINCT ON vs ROW_NUMBER() = 1 vs a MAX subquery.
  Count: 4
  Example pairs: p00663, p00673, p01130, p01715
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Cause: Recursive CTEs seeded differently. A scalar seed vs a joined seed, or
  seeding with the root and filtering it out.
  Count: 2
  Example pairs: p00184, p00840

  Where I'd start, by payoff per effort and how easy it is to keep the rules
  sound:
  1. Group 3: 8 pairs, very low effort. Drop OFFSET 0, and drop LIMIT n (n ≥ 1)
     on an aggregate with no GROUP BY. Both are trivially sound.
  2. Group 5: 6 pairs, small separate rules. The date_trunc one needs care about
     type casts. The others are one-liners.
  3. The COUNT part of group 2: 5 pairs. Rewrite COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE c IS NOT
     NULL) and COUNT(*) - COUNT(c) to a standard form. Filters in WHERE only
     line up when there's no GROUP BY, so that case needs a guard.
  4. Groups 1 and 4: 26 pairs, the most valuable but hardest. They need general
     reasoning about duplicates ("this output is already distinct, so a join can
     become a semi-join"), and unnesting of derived tables. The duplicate check
     from the UNION fix is a starting point. These are also where a false
     positive is easiest to introduce, so each rule needs boundary tests like
     the earlier ones.
  5. Groups 6 and 7: 6 pairs. I'd leave these as known gaps. Matching them means
     proving tie-breaking behavior, or matching seeds that differ structurally,
     and the payoff is small.

  Doing items 1–3 would fix about 19 pairs and raise recall from 93.7% to about
  95.6%. Want me to start there?

      748 +def _drop_noop_row_limits(tree):                              
      749 +    """OFFSET 0 skips nothing. LIMIT n (n >= 1) changes nothin
          +g on a SELECT that aggregates without                         
      750 +    GROUP BY: it returns exactly one row (none if HAVING fails
          +), unless a set-returning function in                         
      751 +    the select list multiplies it."""                         
      752 +    for q in list(tree.find_all(exp.Select, exp.Union, exp.Int
          +ersect, exp.Except)):                                         
      753 +        off = q.args.get("offset")                            
      754 +        if off is not None and _lit_int(off.expression) == 0: 
      755 +            q.set("offset", None)                             
      756 +        lim = q.args.get("limit")                             
      757 +        n = _lit_int(lim.expression) if isinstance(lim, exp.Li
          +mit) else None                                                
      758 +        if not isinstance(q, exp.Select) or n is None or n < 1
          + or q.args.get("offset") or q.args.get("group"):              
      759 +            continue                                          
      760 +        if not any(_owner_select(a) is q for p in q.expression
          +s for a in p.find_all(exp.AggFunc)):                          
      761 +            continue

          + exp.Anonymous) and not isinstance(x.parent, exp.Window))     
      763 +               for p in q.expressions for x in p.walk()):     
      764 +            continue  # an unknown function might return a set
      765 +        if q.find(exp.Window) or (q.args.get("distinct") and q
          +.args["distinct"].args.get("on")):                            
      766 +            continue                                          
      767 +        q.set("limit", None)

⏺ All 8 no-op LIMIT/OFFSET pairs are fixed, with no regressions.

  Item 2: the six small rules. First I'll check how the serializer treats NULLS

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've identified six normalization rules: <> ALL/= ANY map directly to NOT
  IN/IN, COALESCE drops NULL args, date_trunc('year', ...) equality simplifies
  to EXTRACT(YEAR ...), NULLs in an IN list can be stripped only within
  AND/OR-reachable truth contexts (not under NOT), and NULLS FIRST/LAST resets
  to default when the sort key is provably non-NULL.

      1143 +def _drop_null_in_list_items(tree):                          
      1144 +    """In a truth context (a WHERE / HAVING / inner ON condit
           +ion, reached only through AND and OR),                       
      1145 +    a NULL in an IN list can only turn FALSE into NULL, and b
           +oth reject the row: drop it."""

           +it(c.expression))

      1689 +def _scalar_chain_joins(s, outer_aliases, R, ctx):           
      1690 +    """LEFT JOINs equal to scalar subquery s (inner joins of 
           +base tables x1..xn), or None. Each x_k                       
      1691 +    must be fixed to at most one row by key equalities on out
           +er columns, literals and x_(k-1), and                        
      1692 +    from k = 2 on those equalities must use x_(k-1); the valu
           +e must read only x_n. Then a missing                         
      1693 +    link NULL-extends every later table, so the value is NULL
           + exactly when s returns no row, and no                       
      1694 +    join fans out. Each condition goes into the ON of the las
           +t table it mentions."""                                      
      1695 +    srcs = _sources(s)                                       
      1696 +    if any(not isinstance(n, exp.Table) or not R.base_table(a
           +) or (j is not None and j.args.get("side"))

           +here") else []

           +c in conds):

      1705 +    local = {a: n for a, n, _ in srcs}                       
      1706 +    order, known = [], set(outer_aliases)                    
      1707 +    while len(order) < len(local):                           
      1708 +        prev = order[-1] if order else None                  
      1709 +        nxt = None                                           
      1710 +        for a in local:                                      
      1711 +            if a in order:                                   
      1712 +                continue                                     
      1713 +            eq_cols, uses_prev = [], False                   
      1714 +            for c in conds:                                  
      1715 +                if not isinstance(c, exp.EQ):                
      1716 +                    continue                                 
      1717 +                for l, r in ((c.this, c.expression), (c.expre
           +ssion, c.this)):                                             
      1718 +                    if isinstance(l, exp.Column) and l.table 
           +== a:                                                        
      1719 +                        refs = _col_refs(r)                  
      1720 +                        if (refs and all(x.table in known for
           + x in refs)) or \                                            
      1721 +                                (not refs and isinstance(r, (
           +exp.Literal, exp.Cast))):                                    
      1722 +                            eq_cols.append(l.name)

           +for x in refs)

           +d (prev is None or uses_prev):                               
      1725 +                nxt = a                                      
      1726 +                break                                        
      1727 +        if nxt is None:                                      
      1728 +            return None                                      
      1729 +        order.append(nxt)                                    
      1730 +        known.add(nxt)                                       
      1731 +    if any(c.table != order[-1] for c in _col_refs(_unalias(s
           +.expressions[0]))):                                          
      1732 +        return None                                          
      1733 +    rank = {a: i for i, a in enumerate(order)}               
      1734 +    on = {a: [] for a in order}                              
      1735 +    for c in conds:                                          
      1736 +        mine = [rank[x.table] for x in _col_refs(c) if x.tabl
           +e in rank]                                                   
      1737 +        if any(x.table not in rank and x.table not in outer_a
           +liases for x in _col_refs(c)):                               
      1738 +            return None                                      
      1739 +        on[order[max(mine) if mine else 0]].append(c.copy()) 
      1740 +    return [exp.Join(this=local[a].copy(), on=_and_all(on[a])
           +, side="LEFT") for a in order]

      537 +def _select_star_of_setop(tree):                              
      538 +    """SELECT [DISTINCT] <every column, in order> FROM (<set o

      572 +def _distinct_set_branches(tree):                             
      573 +    """A UNION / INTERSECT / EXCEPT without ALL removes duplic
          +ates from its inputs, so a plain                              
      574 +    branch may as well be SELECT DISTINCT (which lets join and
          + EXISTS spellings meet)."""

      585 +def _split_in_intersect(sel):                                 
      586 +    """x IN (A INTERSECT B), as a WHERE conjunct, is x IN (A) 
          +AND x IN (B): membership in both."""                          
      587 +    where = sel.args.get("where")                             
      588 +    if not where:                                             
      589 +        return                                                
      590 +    out, changed = [], False                                  
      591 +    for c in _conjuncts(where.this):                          
      592 +        q = c.args.get("query") if isinstance(c, exp.In) else 
          +None                                                          
      593 +        q = q.this if isinstance(q, exp.Subquery) else q      
      594 +        if isinstance(q, exp.Intersect):                      
      595 +            parts, stack = [], [q]                            
      596 +            while stack:                                      
      597 +                n = stack.pop()                               
      598 +                if isinstance(n, exp.Intersect):              
      599 +                    stack += [n.this, n.expression]           
      600 +                elif isinstance(n, exp.Select) and len(n.expre
          +ssions) == 1:                                                 
      601 +                    parts.append(n)                           
      602 +                else:                                         
      603 +                    parts = None                              
      604 +                    break                                     
      605 +            if parts:                                         
      606 +                out += [exp.In(this=c.this.copy(), query=exp.S
          +ubquery(this=p.copy())) for p in parts]                       
      607 +                changed = True                                
      608 +                continue

⏺ The ORDER BY fix resolved the regression, leaving 34 misses and no false
  positives. I identified that deduplicated foreign-key columns correspond to
  semi-joins, and found two supporting rules (IN-in-OR becoming EXISTS, and
  EXISTS distributing over OR) that let equivalent query forms be rewritten into
  this pattern.

      2209 +def _split_correlated_or_exists(tree):

⏺ I've traced the regressions to an ordering issue: my rule runs before
  decorrelation produces the GROUP BY/HAVING form but after the
  equivalence-class rule renames the grouping key, so it misses both. I'll move
  it to run right after decorrelation and the LEFT-to-INNER steps, but before
  the equivalence-class rewrite, so it catches both forms.

      940 +def _prune_derived_columns(tree):                             
      941 +    """A derived table's columns that nothing reads can go: re
          +moving a column from a SELECT without                         
      942 +    DISTINCT keeps its rows (a lone constant stands in when no
          + column is read)."""                                          
      943 +    read = {}                                                 
      944 +    for c in tree.find_all(exp.Column):                       
      945 +        if c.table:                                           
      946 +            read.setdefault(c.table, set()).add(c.name)       
      947 +    for sub in list(tree.find_all(exp.Subquery)):             
      948 +        q = sub.this                                          
      949 +        if not isinstance(sub.parent, (exp.From, exp.Join)) or
          + not sub.alias or not isinstance(q, exp.Select) \             
      950 +                or q.args.get("distinct") or (sub.args.get("al
          +ias") and sub.args["alias"].args.get("columns")):             
      951 +            continue                                          
      952 +        used = read.get(sub.alias, set())                     
      953 +        keep = [p for p in q.expressions if p.alias_or_name in
          + used]                                                        
      954 +        if len(keep) == len(q.expressions):                   
      955 +            continue                                          
      956 +        q.set("expressions", keep or [exp.alias_(exp.Literal.n
          +umber(1), "_unused", quoted=True)])                           
      957 +    return tree                                               
      958 +                                                              
      959 +

      962      internal to the query; only positions matter)."""

      2397 +def _merge_grouped_derived(tree, R, ctx):                    
      2398 +    """A non-aggregating SELECT over one grouped derived tabl
           +e t = (SELECT g.., AGG.. FROM .. GROUP BY                    
      2399 +    g..) and base tables O (inner joins only, each with a pri
           +mary key) is that grouping done in                           
      2400 +    place: FROM <t's sources> JOIN O WHERE <t's WHERE> AND <o
           +uter conditions not reading t's                              
      2401 +    aggregates> GROUP BY g.., <O's primary keys> HAVING <t's 
           +HAVING> AND <outer conditions reading                        
      2402 +    them>, with t's columns replaced by their definitions. Ea
           +ch group is one (t row, O row                                
      2403 +    combination) pair; conditions linking O to t's grouping k
           +eys are constant within a group, so the                      
      2404 +    aggregates see exactly t's rows for that group."""       
      2405 +    def merge(sel):                                          
      2406 +        if sel.args.get("group") or sel.args.get("having") or
           + sel.args.get("with") \                                      
      2407 +                or (sel.args.get("distinct") and sel.args["di
           +stinct"].args.get("on")) \                                   
      2408 +                or any(_owner_select(a) is sel for a in sel.f
           +ind_all(exp.AggFunc)):                                       
      2409 +            return None

      2411 +        derived = [(a, n, j) for a, n, j in srcs if isinstanc
           +e(n, exp.Subquery)]                                          
      2412 +        if len(derived) != 1 or any(j is not None and j.args.
           +get("side") for _, _, j in srcs):                            
      2413 +            return None                                      
      2414 +        t, sub, _ = derived[0]

      2416 +        if not isinstance(g, exp.Select) or not g.args.get("g
           +roup") or _has_grouping_sets(g) \

           +imit", "offset", "with")) or g.find(exp.Window):             
      2418 +            return None                                      
      2419 +        keys = {k.sql() for k in g.args["group"].expressions}
      2420 +        if not all(isinstance(k, exp.Column) for k in g.args[
           +"group"].expressions):                                       
      2421 +            return None                                      
      2422 +        defs = {p.alias_or_name: _unalias(p) for p in g.expre
           +ssions}                                                      
      2423 +        aggregate_cols = {name for name, d in defs.items() if
           + d.sql() not in keys}                                        
      2424 +        others = [(a, n) for a, n, _ in srcs if a != t]      
      2425 +        for a, n in others:                                  
      2426 +            tname = R.base_table(a) if isinstance(n, exp.Tabl
           +e) else None                                                 
      2427 +            if not tname or not ctx.table(tname).primary_key:
      2428 +                return None                                  
      2429 +        if any(c.table == t and c.name not in defs for c in s
           +el.find_all(exp.Column)):                                    
      2430 +            return None                                      
      2431 +                                                             
      2432 +        def subst(e):                                        
      2433 +            e = e.copy()                                     
      2434 +            for c in list(e.find_all(exp.Column)):           
      2435 +                if c.table == t:                             
      2436 +                    c.replace(defs[c.name].copy())           
      2437 +            return defs[e.name].copy() if isinstance(e, exp.C
           +olumn) and e.table == t else e                               
      2438 +                                                             
      2439 +        def reads_aggregate(e):                              
      2440 +            return any(c.table == t and c.name in aggregate_c
           +ols for c in e.find_all(exp.Column))                         
      2441 +        conds = _conjuncts(sel.args["where"].this) if sel.arg
           +s.get("where") else []                                       
      2442 +        for _, _, j in srcs:                                 
      2443 +            if j is not None:                                
      2444 +                conds += _conjuncts(j.args.get("on"))        
      2445 +        out = g.copy()                                       
      2446 +        out.set("order", None)                               
      2447 +        for a, n in others:                                  
      2448 +            out.append("joins", exp.Join(this=n.copy()))     
      2449 +        where = (_conjuncts(out.args["where"].this) if out.ar
           +gs.get("where") else []) \                                   
      2450 +            + [subst(c) for c in conds if not reads_aggregate
           +(c)]                                                         
      2451 +        having = (_conjuncts(out.args["having"].this) if out.
           +args.get("having") else []) \                                
      2452 +            + [subst(c) for c in conds if reads_aggregate(c)]
      2453 +        _where_set(out, where)                               
      2454 +        out.set("having", exp.Having(this=_and_all(having)) i
           +f having else None)                                          
      2455 +        gkeys = [k.copy() for k in g.args["group"].expression
           +s]

      2457 +            gkeys += [exp.column(k, table=a, quoted=True) for
           + k in ctx.table(R.base_table(a)).primary_key]                
      2458 +        out.set("group", exp.Group(expressions=gkeys))       
      2459 +        out.set("expressions", [exp.alias_(subst(_unalias(p))
           +, p.alias_or_name, quoted=True) if isinstance(p, exp.Alias)  
      2460 +                                else subst(p) for p in sel.ex

      2479 +            break                                            
      2480 +        R.refresh(tree)                                      
      2481 +    return tree

      1553 +def _decorrelate_counts(sel, cands, pks, ctx):               
      1554 +    """Several correlated (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM t x WHERE <co
           +nds>) in the select list: LEFT JOIN each                     
      1555 +    t x ON <conds> and count COUNT(DISTINCT x.<pk>), GROUP BY
           + the outer primary keys. The joins can                       
      1556 +    multiply rows, but each x row is still counted once, and 
           +a NULL-extended x counts 0."""                               
      1557 +    for q, s, agg, x, tname in cands:                        
      1558 +        pk = ctx.table(tname).primary_key                    
      1559 +        if not (isinstance(agg, exp.Count) and isinstance(agg
           +.this, (exp.Star, exp.Literal))) or len(pk) != 1:            
      1560 +            return                                           
      1561 +        if _owner_select(q) is not sel or not any(q is _unali
           +as(p) or q in list(p.find_all(exp.Subquery))                 
      1562 +                                                  for p in se
           +l.expressions):                                              
      1563 +            return                                           
      1564 +        if sel.args.get("where") and any(n is q for n in sel.
           +args["where"].find_all(exp.Subquery)):                       
      1565 +            return                                           
      1566 +    repl = {}                                                
      1567 +    for q, s, agg, x, tname in cands:                        
      1568 +        conds = _conjuncts(s.args["where"].this) if s.args.ge
           +t("where") else []                                           
      1569 +        sel.append("joins", exp.Join(this=s.args["from"].this
           +.copy(), on=_and_all([c.copy() for c in conds]),             
      1570 +                                     side="LEFT"))           
      1571 +        pk = exp.column(ctx.table(tname).primary_key[0], tabl
           +e=x, quoted=True)                                            
      1572 +        repl[q.sql()] = exp.Count(this=exp.Distinct(expressio
           +ns=[pk]), big_int=True)                                      
      1573 +                                                             
      1574 +    def swap(n):                                             
      1575 +        return repl[n.sql()].copy() if isinstance(n, exp.Subq
           +uery) and n.sql() in repl else n                             
      1576 +    sel.set("expressions", [p.transform(swap) for p in sel.ex
           +pressions])                                                  
      1577 +    if sel.args.get("order"):                                
      1578 +        sel.set("order", sel.args["order"].transform(swap))  
      1579 +    sel.set("group", exp.Group(expressions=pks))             
      1580 +                                                             
      1581 +                                                             
      1582  def _left_join_count_having_to_inner(sel, R, ctx):
      1583      """With one group per outer row (GROUP BY covers the othe
            r tables' primary keys), a HAVING
      1584      COUNT(b.c) > k (k >= 0) discards exactly the NULL-extende
            d groups of LEFT JOIN b."""

      223  4. **Canonicalization** (`sqleval/canonical.py`). Each rewrite
            below states the condition that
      224     makes it sound:
      225     - *Names and structure:* parentheses, `FETCH FIRST`/`LIMIT`
           , positional and alias references in
      226 -     `GROUP BY`/`ORDER BY`, `*` expansion, `USING` → `ON`. CTE
          -s used once become derived tables.                            
      227 -     Aliases are made globally unique. CTE and derived-table o
          -utput columns are renamed by                                  
      228 -     position.                                                
      226 +     `GROUP BY`/`ORDER BY`, `*` expansion, `USING` → `ON`. CTE
          +s become derived tables (when read                            
      227 +     more than once, only if deterministic: no `random()`, `ne
          +xtval`, ...). Aliases are made                                
      228 +     globally unique. Derived-table columns nothing reads are 
          +dropped (not under `DISTINCT`), and                           
      229 +     the rest are renamed by position.                        
      230 +   - *Row limits and set operations:*                         
      231 +     - `OFFSET 0` is dropped, and `LIMIT n` (n ≥ 1) on an ungr
          +ouped aggregate (exactly one row).                            
      232 +     - `SELECT [DISTINCT] <all columns> FROM (<set operation>)
          + t` is the set operation; with                                
      233 +       `DISTINCT` its `UNION ALL` chain becomes `UNION`.      
      234 +     - `L EXCEPT R`, with L's columns never NULL → `SELECT [DI
          +STINCT] L … WHERE NOT EXISTS (R …                             
      235 +       AND R = L)`; `DISTINCT` is omitted, and `EXCEPT ALL` is
          + accepted, when L's rows are                                  
      236 +       provably distinct.                                     
      237 +     - A deduplicated set of values that each name one row of 
          +`T` by its key (`T.k` itself, or a                            
      238 +       NOT NULL foreign key to it) → `SELECT t.k FROM T t WHER
          +E …` with a semi-join per source:                             
      239 +       a `UNION` of such branches (or a provably duplicate-fre
          +e `UNION ALL`), `SELECT DISTINCT fk`,                         
      240 +       and `COUNT(DISTINCT fk)` (→ `COUNT(*)`; NULLs need not 
          +be excluded there).                                           
      241 +     - Plain branches of a deduplicating `UNION`/`INTERSECT`/`
          +EXCEPT` are marked `DISTINCT`.                                
      242 +     - A grouped derived table joined to keyed tables, or alon
          +e, is merged into its outer query:                            
      243 +       one `GROUP BY` (plus the other tables' primary keys), o
          +uter conditions on its aggregates in                          
      244 +       `HAVING`. Window functions in the outer query are kept 
          +(they run after grouping).                                    
      245     - *Expressions:*
      246       - Comparisons are oriented consistently, `NOT` is pushed 
           into comparisons, `BETWEEN` is
      231 -       expanded, and single-value `IN` becomes `=`.           
      247 +       expanded, and single-value `IN` becomes `=`. `= ANY (su
          +bquery)` is `IN`, `<> ALL                                     
      248 +       (subquery)` is `NOT IN`.                               
      249 +     - `NULL` items of an `IN` list are dropped in WHERE / HAV
          +ING / ON conditions reached only                              
      250 +       through `AND`/`OR` (where NULL and FALSE both reject th
          +e row), and `NULL` arguments of                               
      251 +       `COALESCE` always.                                     
      252 +     - `date_trunc('year', a) = date_trunc('year', b)` becomes
          + `EXTRACT(YEAR …)` equality.                                  
      253 +     - A window frame with only a start ends at `CURRENT ROW`.
      254       - `IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM NULL` becomes `IS [NOT] NULL`; 
           `CASE WHEN x IS NULL …` becomes
      255         `COALESCE`.
      234 -     - `COUNT(1)` becomes `COUNT(*)`.                         
      256 +     - `COUNT(1)` becomes `COUNT(*)`. After the per-SELECT rew
          +rites, `COUNT(c)` is written                                  
      257 +       `COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE c IS NOT NULL)` and `COUNT(*) -
          + COUNT(c)` is `COUNT(*) FILTER

⏺ Items 1–4 are done. All 46 targeted pairs now match, and the harness still has
  no false positives: accuracy is 99.3% (from 97.0%) and recall 98.5% (from
  93.7%). 15 misses remain, all in groups you didn't ask me to take on.

  Item: 1. No-op LIMIT/OFFSET
  Pairs fixed: 8/8
  What it took: Drop OFFSET 0, and drop LIMIT n on an aggregate with no GROUP
  BY.
  Kept when there's an OFFSET or a function that could return several rows.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Item: 2. Small rules
  Pairs fixed: 6/6
  What it took: = ANY / <> ALL as IN / NOT IN; NULLs in IN lists
  (WHERE/HAVING/ON
  only, never under NOT) and in COALESCE; date_trunc('year') equality; NULLS
  FIRST/LAST on non-NULL keys; scalar subqueries over a chain of key joins.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Item: 3. COUNT forms
  Pairs fixed: 5/5, plus p02004
  What it took: Standard COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE …) form. The filter moves into
  WHERE only when there's no GROUP BY and every aggregate has the same filter.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Item: 4. Set ops / semi-joins (group 1)
  Pairs fixed: 18/18
  What it took: DISTINCT over set operations; general EXCEPT → NOT EXISTS with a

  distinctness check based on keys; IN (A INTERSECT B); sets of foreign-key
  values as semi-joins (UNION, DISTINCT, COUNT(DISTINCT)); IN → EXISTS inside
  OR; splitting EXISTS over OR; the two GROUP BY … HAVING counting patterns.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Item: 4. Pre-aggregation (group 4)
  Pairs fixed: 8/8
  What it took: Merging grouped derived tables into the outer query, including
  window functions; inlining CTEs read more than once; dropping unread derived
  columns; default window-frame ends; several correlated COUNTs as
  COUNT(DISTINCT pk).

  Soundness checks:
  - I added 54 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py. Each rule has a "should
    match" case next to look-alikes that must not match, aimed at how that rule
    could go wrong. Examples: a LIMIT with GROUP BY, NULL inside a NOT IN list,
    an EXCEPT whose right side has NULLs, a three-column key, a LEFT JOIN of a
    derived table, and plain counts across several LEFT JOINs.
  - All tests pass, and every "must not match" case is rejected on meaning, not
    because its SQL failed to parse.
  - The stress run gives 0 false matches out of 611 breaking mutations, and all
    1,636 equivalence rewrites are recognized.
  - Output is identical with 1 or 10 workers. Scoring the full set takes 0.94 s,
    up from 0.66 s.

  Regressions I hit and fixed along the way:
  - Automatic "add ORDER BY 1" rewrites at first blocked the new EXCEPT, UNION
    and COUNT(DISTINCT) rules. Now they carry ORDER BY and LIMIT over.
  - Splitting an EXISTS copied the subquery aliases, so the serializer numbered
    both copies the same.
  - My HAVING COUNT rule ran at the wrong point in the rewrite pass and missed
    the decorrelated forms.

  Conventions you should know about:
  - CTEs read more than once are inlined only when deterministic. That relies on
    a list of volatile functions such as random() and nextval, so a volatile
    function missing from the list could slip through.
  - LIMIT removal is skipped whenever the query calls a function sqlglot doesn't
    recognize, in case it returns several rows.

  The 15 left:
  - 9 algebraic identities: linearity of SUM, FILTER vs CASE inside SUM, average
    of per-group totals vs a ratio of totals, and a redundant cast.
  - 4 top-1-per-group forms.
  - 2 recursive CTEs seeded differently.

  The README lists them, and its rule list and numbers are updated. Nothing is
  committed yet. The changes are in sqleval/canonical.py, the tests file and
  README.md.

❯ What's causing the issues with the remaining 15

⏺ All 15 are equivalent pairs that need reasoning the harness doesn't have yet.
  Nothing is wrongly labeled and nothing is a harness bug: each pair's labeling
  note checks out against the schema. They fall into three groups.

  1. Algebraic identities (9 pairs)

  The two queries compute the same value with different arithmetic. The
  canonicalizer compares structure, and has no rules for rearranging aggregate
  arithmetic.

  Pair: p01165, p01337, p01489
  Ground truth vs prediction: SUM(a - b) vs SUM(a) - SUM(b) (also
  SUM(a*(1-c/100)) vs SUM(a) - SUM(a*c/100))
  Why it's equal: SUM is linear, as long as no term can be NULL
  What a fix needs: Prove every term non-NULL. p01337 instead uses COALESCE to
  handle the NULL. On an empty table both sides are NULL.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01943, p02005
  Ground truth vs prediction: SUM(CASE WHEN p THEN x ELSE 0 END) vs SUM(x)
  FILTER
  (WHERE p), or vs COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE p)
  Why it's equal: Equal when every group has at least one row
  What a fix needs: Only safe with a plain GROUP BY, where no group is empty.
  With no GROUP BY, an empty table gives NULL on one side and 0 on the other.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01097
  Ground truth vs prediction: SUM(x) / COUNT(DISTINCT o) vs AVG of per-o
  subtotals
  Why it's equal: Same ratio
  What a fix needs: Unnest a derived table under an outer aggregate. Today's
  merge only handles a non-aggregating outer query.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01681
  Ground truth vs prediction: AVG(per-post like count) (LEFT JOIN, GROUP BY) vs
  COUNT(likes)::numeric / NULLIF(COUNT(posts), 0)
  Why it's equal: Every like references a post
  What a fix needs: The same unnesting, plus foreign-key reasoning that the
  likes
  total equals the sum of per-post counts.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p00130
  Ground truth vs prediction: (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM s) / (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM
  i)
  vs SELECT COUNT(*) / (SELECT …) FROM s
  Why it's equal: Both return one row with the same value
  What a fix needs: Turning an uncorrelated scalar COUNT subquery into an
  aggregate over its table.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01454
  Ground truth vs prediction: AVG(smallint::numeric) vs AVG(smallint)
  Why it's equal: AVG of a smallint already returns numeric
  What a fix needs: Type information for aggregate return types, to know the
  cast
  does nothing.

  These are the most fixable of the 15. The first two rows (5 pairs) are small
  rules with clear guards. The other four each need something bigger.

  2. Top-1 per group (4 pairs)

  p00663, p00673, p01130, p01715: DISTINCT ON (k) … ORDER BY k, x DESC vs
  ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY k ORDER BY x DESC) = 1 vs = (SELECT MAX(x) …).

  - Different query shapes. The canonical forms look nothing alike: one is a
    DISTINCT ON, one a filtered window function over a derived table, one a
    correlated MAX subquery.
  - Ties. When rows tie on x, DISTINCT ON and ROW_NUMBER each pick an arbitrary
    row. They agree only if the tie-break is identical (p00663 includes "Id" in
    both), or a uniqueness constraint rules out ties (p00673's
    UNIQUE(EmployeeId, EffectiveDate)). p01130 and p01715 have no tie-break, so
    both queries are nondeterministic, and the labels rely on treating "both
    arbitrary" as equivalent.
  - Effort. A sound fix would rewrite ROW_NUMBER … = 1 into DISTINCT ON only
    when the window's ORDER BY ends in a key, and handle the MAX form through
    key reasoning. That's doable for p00663 and p00673. Matching p01130 and
    p01715 would mean adopting the "both arbitrary" convention, which is a
    policy decision, not a proof.

  3. Recursive CTEs seeded differently (2 pairs)

  - p00184: the seed is JOIN courses c … WHERE c.code = 'CS301' vs course_id =
    (SELECT id … WHERE code = 'CS301'), the outer query uses JOIN prereqs vs id
    IN (SELECT …), and the CTE has a column list. The existing
    scalar-subquery-to-join rule doesn't run inside a recursive CTE's seed. Even
    after that, the outer JOIN vs IN would need the CTE's rows to be distinct,
    which follows from UNION.
  - p00840: seeding with the parent itself (SELECT 100) and filtering WHERE id
    <> 100 afterwards, vs seeding with its children. That's equal only on
    acyclic data, and only because no row's id can be 100 except the root.
    Proving it means reasoning about what the recursion produces, which no
    current rule does.

  Recommendation

  The SUM-linearity and CASE/FILTER-under-GROUP-BY rules would fix 5 pairs with
  low risk. After that, the top-1 forms with a key tie-break (p00663, p00673)
  are the next sound wins. I'd leave the rest as documented limitations, unless
  you want to adopt the "both nondeterministic counts as equal" convention for
  p01130 and p01715.

❯ Lets do 1 and 2 and leave 3 for later

⏺ I found two bugs: the ROW_NUMBER rule's find(exp.Window) check incorrectly
  searches inside derived tables, causing it to always bail out, and the MAX
  rule rejects queries with an ORDER BY that's actually safe to strip early.
  I'll fix the ROW_NUMBER check to only look at the outer select list, and move
  the ORDER BY stripping (for cases without LIMIT/OFFSET/DISTINCT ON) to before
  the rewrites as well as after.

⏺ A false positive appeared. I'll look at exactly what p00664 is before touching

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've confirmed this is a genuine false positive: converting an unordered
  ROW_NUMBER query to DISTINCT ON … ORDER BY inadvertently creates row ordering,
  causing a false match against ordered ground truths like p00664. I'll add a
  guard so this conversion (and the similarly affected correlated-MAX rule) is
  skipped at the outermost query when the comparison is order-sensitive and the
  prediction lacks its own ORDER BY.

⏺ Groups 1 and 2 are done. Eleven of the 13 pairs now match, and the harness
  still has no false positives: accuracy is 99.8%, recall 99.6%, with 4 misses
  left.

  Group 1, algebraic identities: 7 of 9 fixed
  - SUM linearity (p01337, p01489): products are multiplied out inside SUM, and
    SUM(a ± b) splits into separate SUMs. This only applies when every term is a
    non-NULL integer or numeric; floats are excluded because their sums depend
    on order.
  - CASE inside SUM vs FILTER (p01943, p02005):
    - SUM(CASE WHEN p THEN x END) becomes SUM(x) FILTER (WHERE p), which always
      holds.
    - With ELSE 0, it becomes COALESCE(SUM(x) FILTER (WHERE p), 0), or COUNT(*)
      FILTER (WHERE p) when x is 1. The same goes for SUM(COALESCE(x, 0)). These
      only apply where an empty input can't show, because there one side is
      NULL and the other 0.
  - Average of group totals (p01097): AVG over per-group SUMs becomes SUM(x) /
    COUNT(DISTINCT k). That needs k and x non-NULL, and an integer x is cast to
    numeric to match AVG's result type.
  - Scalar subquery next to an aggregate (p00130): each aggregate is written as
    its own scalar subquery.
  - Redundant cast (p01454): AVG(int_col::numeric) is the same as AVG(int_col).

  Group 2, first row per group: 4 of 4 fixed. ROW_NUMBER() = 1 and a correlated
  MAX subquery both become DISTINCT ON. The MAX form also needs a unique NOT
  NULL key, so the latest row can't tie.

  p01130 and p01715 have no tie-break, so both of their queries are
  nondeterministic. They match under the README's existing rule that two queries
  with the same ORDER BY count as equal despite ties. I extended that rule's
  text to cover this case.

  A false positive I caught and fixed: the ROW_NUMBER rewrite briefly matched
  p00664, an unordered prediction against an ordered ground truth. DISTINCT ON's
  ORDER BY also orders the output, so converting an unordered outermost query
  invented an ordering. Both new rules now skip that case, and it has its own
  test.

  The two group-1 pairs I left:
  - p01165 distributes a division, Q*P*(1 - C/100) vs Q*P*C/100. PostgreSQL's
    NUMERIC division rounds based on the magnitude of the number being divided,
    so the two can differ for very large values. They're equal on realistic
    data, but not on every valid database.
  - p01681 would need several new rules chained together. The obvious rewrite
    also divides by zero on an empty table, where AVG returns NULL.

  Checks:
  - I added 24 test cases to tests/test_equivalence.py, each pairing a "should
    match" case with look-alikes that must not match. All tests pass, and every
    "must not match" case is rejected on meaning, not because its SQL failed to
    parse.
  - The stress run gives 0 false matches out of 611 breaking mutations, and all
    1,636 equivalence rewrites are recognized.
  - Output is identical with 1 or 10 workers. Scoring the full set takes 1.02 s.
  - I updated README.md with the rules, numbers, conventions and remaining gaps.

  The 4 misses left are group 3 (p00184, p00840, deferred as you asked) plus
  p01165 and p01681. Nothing is committed yet.

❯ What's the category of the remaining 4 misses

  hand-written equivalent pairs, not automatic rewrites. By cause, they fall
  into three groups:

  Pair: p00184

  Cause: Recursive CTE seeded differently (group 3, deferred)
  Why it isn't matched: The seed uses a scalar subquery instead of a join, and
  the outer query uses IN instead of JOIN. The scalar-subquery-to-join rule
  doesn't run inside a recursive CTE's seed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  Query: project_tracker_006
  Cause: Recursive CTE seeded differently (group 3, deferred)
  Why it isn't matched: It seeds with the root task and filters it out with id

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01165
  Query: sales_snowflake_012
  Cause: Distributed division (algebraic identity)
  Why it isn't matched: Q*P*(1 - C/100) vs Q*P*C/100. PostgreSQL's NUMERIC
  division rounds based on the size of the number being divided, so the two can
   differ for very large values. Left unmatched on purpose.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Pair: p01681

  Cause: Average vs ratio of table counts (algebraic identity)

  NULLIF(COUNT(posts), 0). Matching it needs a foreign-key argument that the
  per-post counts add up to the likes total, plus handling of division by zero
  on an empty table. Left unmatched on purpose.
