❯ Whats the reasoning for leaving the two algebraic ones unmatched?

⏺ They were left for different reasons. p01165 can't be proven sound, and the
  label is arguably too generous. p01681 is genuinely equivalent but expensive
  to prove.

  p01165: the division rounds differently

  GT: SUM(Quantity * UnitPrice * (1 - COALESCE(DiscountPct, 0) / 100))
  PR: SUM(Quantity * UnitPrice) - SUM(Quantity * UnitPrice *
  COALESCE(DiscountPct, 0) / 100)

  On paper these are equal. In PostgreSQL they aren't guaranteed equal, because
  NUMERIC division doesn't keep unlimited digits. It aims for about 16
  significant digits, and never keeps fewer decimal places than its inputs have.

  - GT divides only DiscountPct / 100. DiscountPct is NUMERIC(5,2), under 1000,
    so the quotient is tiny and keeps all its decimals. After that, everything
    is multiplication and addition, which are exact for NUMERIC. The GT is
    exact.
  - PR divides (Quantity * UnitPrice * DiscountPct) / 100. That dividend has 4
    decimal places, and an exact quotient needs 6. Once the quotient has more
    than about 10 digits before the decimal point (a dividend above roughly
    10¹²), the 16-digit budget runs out and the result gets rounded.

  The column types allow it: Quantity is an INTEGER up to about 2.1 × 10⁹,
  UnitPrice is NUMERIC(10,2) up to about 10⁸, and DiscountPct is under 10³. So a
  schema-valid row can make the two queries give different sums.

  The harness promises never to match two queries unless they agree on every
  database the schema allows. The README already says rewrites that distribute
  division aren't performed, for exactly this reason. Matching p01165 would
  break that promise. The column precisions are the only magnitude information
  available, and they allow values large enough to round, so no guard could
  rescue it.

  Equal on realistic data, but not provably equal. Your options:
  - Keep it as a known limitation (the current state).
  - Relabel it "not equivalent", in the same spirit as the p00582 correction.
  - Adopt a "values compared ignoring NUMERIC rounding" convention. That would
    contradict the README's current rule, and other rewrites would then need
    revisiting too.

  p01681: equivalent, but it would take four new rules for one pair

  GT: SELECT AVG(cnt) FROM (SELECT p.id, COUNT(l."userId") AS cnt
                            FROM posts p LEFT JOIN "postLikes" l ON l."postId" =
  p.id GROUP BY p.id) t
  PR: SELECT (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM "postLikes")::numeric / NULLIF((SELECT
  COUNT(*) FROM posts), 0)

  These really are equal: every like references a post, and both give NULL when
  there are no posts. Nothing blocks a proof. I left it because the proof needs
  four separate rewrites, each with its own conditions and false-positive risks,
  to fix one pair:

  1. AVG over per-group COUNTs as a ratio. The p01097 rule I added only covers
     SUM. With COUNT, the obvious form COUNT(…)::numeric / COUNT(DISTINCT p.id)
     errors with division by zero on an empty table, where AVG returns NULL. The
     rule would have to produce NULLIF(…, 0), a new shape the harness doesn't
     use anywhere else.
  2. FK counting. It needs proof that COUNT(l."userId") over posts LEFT JOIN
     postLikes equals COUNT(*) of postLikes. That holds only because postId is a
     NOT NULL foreign key, so no like is lost.
  3. COUNT(DISTINCT p.id) over that LEFT JOIN equals COUNT(*) of posts. This
     depends on the LEFT JOIN preserving every post.
  4. Two independent table counts. The two counts in the prediction come from
     separate scalar subqueries over unrelated tables, while the ground truth
     computes both from one joined query. The scalar-subquery rule from p00130
     goes the other way and doesn't cover this.

  Each step is about as complicated as the whole p01097 rule, needs its own
  boundary tests, and so far fits only this one pair. For one pair out of 2,046,
  I judged the false-positive risk not worth it. That's a cost/benefit call,
  not a soundness limit, so if you want it matched I can build it the same way
  as the others, with tests for each step.
